) shows that
C_{3}\implies\left((F_{pq}(1)=0)\land(F_{pq}(2)=0)\land(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\neq 0)\right).

The result therefore follows from an application of Lemma
B.5
using the assignments

x=g^{a}_{p-q}
,
y=g^{b}_{p-q}
and
z=g^{c}_{p-q}
, having noted the definitions of
F_{pq}(1)
,
F_{pq}(2)
and
F^{abc}_{pq}(3)
in (
121
) to (
123
).
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

When
C_{3}
is satisfied, it is necessarily the case that one of the elements of
\{\epsilon^{ab}_{pq},\epsilon^{bc}_{pq},\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}\}
is zero while the other two sum to zero but are themselves non-zero.

Proof.

Inspection of (
301
) shows that
C_{3}\implies\left((\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)=0)\land(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)=0)\land(\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\neq 0)\right).

The result therefore follows from an application of Lemma
B.5
using the assignments

x=\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}
,
y=\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}
and
z=\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}
, having noted the definitions of
\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)
,
\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)
and
\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)
in (
114
) to (
116
).
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

For an event satisfying
C_{3}
:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\left((f(b,c;a)\neq 0)\land(g^{a}_{p-q}=0)\right)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\lor\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
\left((f(c,a;b)\neq 0)\land(g^{b}_{p-q}=0)\right)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\lor\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
\left((f(a,b;c)\neq 0)\land(g^{c}_{p-q}=0)\right)\end{array}\right]\implies\left[\mathscr{P}(f)\neq 0\right].

Proof.

For an event satisfying
C_{3}
Lemma
5.10
has already shown us that one of the elements of
\{g^{a}_{p-q},g^{b}_{p-q},g^{c}_{p-q}\}
is zero while the other two are non-zero. The
g
which vanishes will ‘switch off’ four of the six terms in the sum which defines
\mathscr{P}(f)
in (
5.8
). Suppose, without loss of generality, that
g^{b}_{p-q}=0
and

\displaystyle g^{a}_{p-q}\neq

\displaystyle 0\neq g^{c}_{p-q}

(303)

In such a case:

\displaystyle\left.\vphantom{\int}\mathscr{P}(f)\right|_{g^{b}_{p-q}=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Perms of}\\
\{a,c\}\end{array}}\left\{\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\cdot F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\cdot g^{a}_{p-q}\cdot g^{c}_{p-q}f(a,c;b)\vphantom{\int}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\cdot g^{a}_{p-q}\cdot g^{c}_{p-q}\cdot\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Perms of}\\
\{a,c\}\end{array}}\left\{F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\cdot f(a,c;b)\vphantom{\int}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\cdot g^{a}_{p-q}\cdot g^{c}_{p-q}\cdot\left\{F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\cdot f(a,c;b)+F^{cba}_{pq}(3)\cdot f(c,a;b)\vphantom{\int}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\cdot g^{a}_{p-q}\cdot g^{c}_{p-q}\cdot\left\{F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\cdot f(a,c;b)+(-F^{abc}_{pq}(3))\cdot(-f(a,c;b))\vphantom{\int}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\cdot g^{a}_{p-q}\cdot g^{c}_{p-q}\cdot 2\cdot F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\cdot f(a,c;b).

(304)

We recognise, therefore, that in such a case
\mathscr{P}(f)
is non-zero if
f(a,c;b)
is non-zero since the remaining terms in
the product (
304
) are non-zero either due to (
303
) or due to constraints in
C_{3}
.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

For an any
collision event

e
in
\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
the following result holds:

\displaystyle{}C_{3}

\displaystyle\implies\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{172.5pt}{1.42262pt}}\\
(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)=0)\land(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)=0)\land(\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(F_{pq}(2)=0)\land(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{172.5pt}{1.42262pt}}\end{array}\right]{}

\displaystyle\implies\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\left[(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=0)\land(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}\neq 0)\land\left\{\text{$(\vec{a}-\vec{b})\propto\vec{p}$ in the $(p+q)$ rest frame}\right\}\right]\lor\\
\qquad\left[(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=0)\land(\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\neq 0)\land\left\{\text{$(\vec{b}-\vec{c})\propto\vec{p}$ in the $(p+q)$ rest frame}\right\}\right]\lor\\
\qquad\qquad\left[(\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=0)\land(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\neq 0)\land\left\{\text{$(\vec{c}\vphantom{\vec{b}}-\vec{a})\propto\vec{p}$ in the $(p+q)$ rest frame}\right\}\right]\end{array}\right].

Proof.

The existence of the first implication has already been noted in the proofs of Lemmas
5.10
and
5.12
.
Regarding the second implication:
Lemma
5.10

tells us that of the elements of
\{g^{a}_{p-q},g^{b}_{p-q},g^{c}_{p-q}\}
, exactly one is zero, while the other two are neither zero nor equal to each other. Since the statement of the lemma which we are trying to prove is invariant under any permutation of
a
,
b
and
c
we may assume,
without loss of generality
, that

\displaystyle g^{b}_{p-q}=0\qquad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\qquad 0\neq g^{a}_{p-q}\neq g^{c}_{p-q}\neq 0.

(305)

Lemma
5.12

tells us that of the elements of
\{\epsilon^{ab}_{pq},\epsilon^{bc}_{pq},\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}\}
only one is zero, while the other two
sum
to zero but are not zero themselves.
There are then three separate possibilities for the epsilons, each of which can be parameterized by some
Y\in\mathbb{R}
with
Y\neq 0
:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c!{:}c!{\text{and}}c}\text{Case $a$}&\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=0&\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=Y,\\
\text{Case $b$}&\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=0&\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=Y,\\
\text{Case $c$}&\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=0&\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=Y.\end{array}\right]

The result of the Lemma
5.14
will be proved if it is proved in each of the three cases listed above. The proof of the Lemma for ‘Case
b
’ will proceed differently to those for ‘Case
a
’ and ‘Case
c
’
on account of our having made
b
special in (
305
). Proofs in each of the three sub-cases follow. Each proof proceeds in the
(p+q)
rest-frame, which Lemma
3.27
tells us always exists.

Case
a
.

Making use of Lemma
3.35
this case asserts that

\displaystyle\vec{b}\times\vec{c}\cdot\vec{p}

\displaystyle=0,

(309)

\displaystyle\vec{c}\times\vec{a}\cdot\vec{p}

\displaystyle=X,

(310)

\displaystyle\vec{b}\times\vec{a}\cdot\vec{p}

\displaystyle=X

(311)

for some
X\in\mathbb{R}
with
X\neq 0
. Subtracting the last two equations gives:

\displaystyle(\vec{c}-\vec{b})\times\vec{a}\cdot\vec{p}=0.

(312)

This tells us that
(\vec{c}-\vec{b})
,
\vec{a}
and
\vec{p}
are linearly dependent,
i.e.
there exists some constants
\sigma,\lambda,\mu
(not all zero) such that

\displaystyle\sigma(\vec{c}-\vec{b})+\lambda\vec{a}+\mu\vec{p}=0.

(313)

If
\lambda\neq 0
then we would have

\vec{a}=\frac{\sigma}{\lambda}(\vec{b}-\vec{c})-\frac{\mu}{\lambda}\vec{p}

from which we could deduce that

\vec{a}\times\vec{c}\cdot\vec{p}=\frac{\sigma}{\lambda}\vec{b}\times\vec{c}\cdot\vec{p}=0\text{\qquad(using \eqref{eq:bcp0})}

which would contradict (
310
). It must therefore be the case that
\lambda=0
. As
\lambda=0
, we know that: (i)
\sigma
and
\mu
are not both zero; and (ii) equation (
313
) can be re-written as:

\displaystyle\sigma(\vec{c}-\vec{b})+\mu\vec{p}=0.

(314)

Neither
\vec{c}-\vec{b}
nor
\vec{p}
is the zero vector (since
\vec{c}=\vec{b}
would contradict (
305
) while
\vec{p}=0
would contradict both (
310
) and the assumption that
e
is a
collision event
) so both
\sigma

and

\mu
must be non-zero. Therefore

\displaystyle\vec{c}-\vec{b}=-\frac{\mu}{\sigma}\vec{p}\qquad\qquad\text{($\sigma\neq 0$,$\mu\neq 0$).}

(315)

∎

Case
b
.

This case asserts that

\displaystyle\vec{c}\times\vec{a}\cdot\vec{p}

\displaystyle=0,

(316)

\displaystyle\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{p}

\displaystyle=X,

(317)

\displaystyle\vec{c}\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{p}

\displaystyle=X

(318)

for some
X\in\mathbb{R}
with
X\neq 0
. Subtracting the last two equations gives:

\displaystyle(\vec{a}-\vec{c})\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{p}=0.

(319)

This tells us that
(\vec{a}-\vec{c})
,
\vec{b}
and
\vec{p}
are linearly dependent, i.e. there exists some constants
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
(not all zero) such that

\displaystyle\alpha(\vec{a}-\vec{c})+\beta\vec{b}+\gamma\vec{p}=0.

(320)

We will now prove that
\gamma\neq 0
by assuming that
\gamma=0
and getting a contradiction. If it were the case that
\gamma=0
we could re-write (
320
) as:

\displaystyle\alpha(\vec{c}-\vec{a})=\beta\vec{b}

(321)

together with the new requirement that at least one of
\alpha
and
\beta
is non-zero. Note, however, that neither
\vec{c}-\vec{a}
nor
\vec{b}
is the zero vector (since
\vec{a}=\vec{c}
would contradict (
305
) while
\vec{p}=0
would contradict both (
317
) and the assumption that
e
is a
collision event
) so having one of
\alpha
and
\beta
non-zero and the other zero is impossible. We can therefore say that
\gamma=0
implies
\alpha\neq 0
and
\beta\neq 0
. Evaluating
G\!\left(\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c@{,\ \!}c}{\bullet}&{p+q}\\
{p-q}&{p+q}\end{array}\!\!\!\!\right)
on each side of (
321
), and using the linear properties of Gram Determinants, gives:

\displaystyle\alpha g^{c}_{p-q}-\alpha g^{a}_{p-q}=\beta g^{b}_{p-q}

(322)

which (using (
305
)) may be re-written as:

\displaystyle\alpha\left(g^{c}_{p-q}-g^{a}_{p-q}\right)=0

(323)

which contradicts either (
305
) or the already established requirements that
\alpha\neq 0
. Accordingly, from this contradiction we conclude that
\gamma\neq 0
as originally desired.

Next we will prove that
\alpha\neq 0
, also by contradiction. If it were the case that
\alpha=0
, then since we now know that
\gamma\neq 0
we could re-write (
320
) as
\vec{p}=-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\vec{b}
which would contradict (
317
). Accordingly
\alpha\neq 0
as required.

Knowing that
\alpha\neq 0
and
\gamma\neq 0
we may now rewrite (
320
) as

\displaystyle\vec{a}-\vec{c}=-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\vec{b}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\vec{p}\qquad\text{($\alpha\neq 0,\gamma\neq 0$)}

(324)

so that

\displaystyle\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{c}

\displaystyle=(\vec{a}-\vec{c})\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{c}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\vec{b}-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\vec{p}\right)\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{c}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\vec{p}\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{c}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}X\qquad\text{(by \eqref{eq:cbpX})}

(325)

which is non-zero since all of
\gamma
,
\alpha
and
X
are themselves non-zero.

We will now show that
\beta=0
. To do this, note that (
324
) can be arranged to read:

\displaystyle\vec{p}=\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}(\vec{c}-\vec{a})-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\vec{b}\qquad\qquad\text{($\alpha\neq 0,\gamma\neq 0$)}

(326)

and thus

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\vec{c}\times\vec{a}\cdot\vec{p}

(from (
316
))

\displaystyle=\vec{c}\times\vec{a}\cdot\left(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}(\vec{c}-\vec{a})-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\vec{b}\right)\qquad

(using (
326
))

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\vec{c}\times\vec{a}\cdot\vec{b}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\beta X}{\gamma\alpha}

(using (
325
))

\displaystyle\implies(\beta=0)

(since
X\neq 0
,
\gamma\neq 0
and
\alpha\neq 0
).

(327)

Putting the above result together with (
324
) it is seen that in ‘Case
b
’ it is always the case that:

\displaystyle\vec{a}-\vec{c}=-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\vec{p}\qquad\text{($\alpha\neq 0,\gamma\neq 0$).}

(328)

∎

Case
c
.

The proof of the lemma for ‘Case
c
’ is exactly the same as the proof of the lemma for ‘Case
a
’ but with
a\leftrightarrow c
.
∎

This concludes the proof of Lemma
5.14
.
∎

Corollary 5.15
.

For an any
collision event

e
in
\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
the following result holds:

\displaystyle C_{3}\implies\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{172.5pt}{1.42262pt}}\\
(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)=0)\land(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)=0)\land(\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(F_{pq}(2)=0)\land(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{172.5pt}{1.42262pt}}\end{array}\right]{}\implies([a,b,c,(p+q)]\neq 0).

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
5.14
was divided into three cases: (a), (b) and (c).
In (
315
) of case (a) it was seen that

\displaystyle\vec{c}-\vec{b}=-\frac{\mu}{\sigma}\vec{p}\qquad\qquad\text{($\sigma\neq 0$,$\mu\neq 0$)}

(329)

which shows that

\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{c}=\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\cdot(\vec{c}-\vec{b})=\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\cdot(-\frac{\mu}{\sigma}\vec{p})=\frac{\mu}{\sigma}\vec{b}\times\vec{a}\cdot\vec{p}=\frac{\mu}{\sigma}X\qquad\text{(by \eqref{eq:bapX})}

which is non-zero since none of
\mu
,
\sigma
or
X
is zero.
In (
325
) of case (b) it was already observed that
\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{c}\neq 0
.
Case (c) would proceed exactly as case (a) but with
a\leftrightarrow c
.
In all cases, therefore, we have seen that
\vec{a}\times\vec{b}\cdot\vec{c}\neq 0
. This, together with Lemma
3.35
concludes the proof.
∎

Definition 5.16

(
C_{3}
brace shorthand
)
.

Symbolically, we shall denote the curly braced part of (
302
), reproduced here:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(f^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a+b}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(a-b,p,q)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
(f^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b+c}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(b-c,p,q)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
(f^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c+a}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(c-a,p,q)\neq 0),\end{array}\right\}

with this symbol:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr&&\end{array}\right\}.

We will fill this symbol with ticks (
✓
), crosses (
✗
) or circles (
\circ
) to indicate which of the corresponding conditions are true, false or unconstrained respectively. For example,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\text{✓}}&{\circ}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\circ}&{\text{✗}}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\circ}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\}

would mean that
(f^{ab}\neq 0)
and
(\Delta_{3}(b-c,p,q)=0)
, with the state of all other constraints unspecified.

Lemma 5.17
.

If the constraints of
C_{3}
in (
302
) are satisfied, then the
brace shorthand
of Definition
5.16
must take one of the following six forms:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\text{✓}}&{\text{✗}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✗}}&{\text{✓}}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\text{✓}}&{\text{✗}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✗}}&{\text{✓}}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\text{✓}}&{\text{✗}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✗}}&{\text{✓}}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\},

which may be summarised more succinctly as

\left[\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\text{✓}}&{\text{✗}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✗}}&{\text{✓}}\end{array}\right\},\text{and all row permutations thereof}\right].

Proof.

That the centre column need always contain two ticks and one cross is easy to show. Lemma
5.11
tells us that of the elements of
\{g^{a}_{p-q},g^{b}_{p-q},g^{c}_{p-q},\}
only one is zero, while the other two
sum
to zero but are not zero themselves. Suppose, without loss of generality, that
g^{b}_{p-q}=0
while the other two are non-zero but sum to zero
g^{a}_{p-q}+g^{c}_{p-q}=0
. In such a case it is trivial to see that the central column of constraints is:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}g^{a+b}_{p-q}\neq 0\\
g^{b+c}_{p-q}\neq 0\\
g^{c+a}_{p-q}\neq 0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c!{\ne}l}g^{a}_{p-q}\hfil\lx@intercol\neq&0\\
g^{c}_{p-q}\hfil\lx@intercol\neq&0\\
0\hfil\lx@intercol\neq&0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\text{✓}\\
\text{✓}\\
\text{✗}\end{array}\right).

The proof is completed by noting that in order for the
C_{1}
constraints to be satisfied, there must be at least one tick in every row of the
brace shorthand
.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

V_{6}
of (
5.9
) is non-zero when the
C_{3}
constraint of (
302
) is satisfied by an event having one of the following three types:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✗}}&{\text{✓}}\end{array}\right\}_{D},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✗}}&{\text{✓}}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\}_{E},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✗}}&{\text{✓}}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\}_{F}.

Proof.

If we can show the result for
\{\phantom{M}\}_{D}
it will follow for
\{\phantom{M}\}_{E}
and
\{\phantom{M}\}_{F}
by symmetry.
The proof of Lemma
5.17
has already shown that the central column of
\{\phantom{M}\}_{D}
implies that

g^{b}_{p-q}=0

and so Lemma
5.13
tells us that
V_{6}
will be non-zero if we can show that
\left(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\right)^{2}-\left(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\right)^{2}\neq 0
when
\{\phantom{M}\}_{D}
pertains.
To prove that
\left(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\right)^{2}-\left(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\right)^{2}
is not zero, we use Lemma
5.14
. This lemma tells us that we may choose to divide the problem into three sub-cases, one for each of the line in this expression:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\left[(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=0)\land(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}\neq 0)\land\left\{\text{$(\vec{a}-\vec{b})\propto\vec{p}$ in the $(p+q)$ rest frame}\right\}\right]\lor\\
\qquad\left[(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=0)\land(\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\neq 0)\land\left\{\text{$(\vec{b}-\vec{c})\propto\vec{p}$ in the $(p+q)$ rest frame}\right\}\right]\lor\\
\qquad\qquad\left[(\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=0)\land(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\neq 0)\land\left\{\text{$(\vec{c}\vphantom{\vec{b}}-\vec{a})\propto\vec{p}$ in the $(p+q)$ rest frame}\right\}\right]\end{array}\right].

In the case shown in the
first
line of (
5.18
) we see that

\left(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\right)^{2}-\left(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\right)^{2}=-\left(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\right)^{2}\neq 0
as desired.

In the case shown in the
second
line of (
5.18
) we see that

\left(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\right)^{2}-\left(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\right)^{2}=\left(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\right)^{2}\neq 0
as desired.

The case shown in the
third
line of (
5.18
) is more problematic, however. Naïve substitution gives

\left(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\right)^{2}-\left(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\right)^{2}=0
which is not the result we desire. We must show, therefore, that the case shown in the
third
line above is incompatible with
\{\phantom{M}\}_{D}
and is therefore impossible.
Specifically, the incompatibility comes from the tick in the bottom right hand corner of
\{\phantom{M}\}_{D}
. This tick asserts that
\Delta_{3}(a-c,p,q)\neq 0
which,
given Lemma
3.36
, tells us that
\vec{a}-\vec{c}
cannot be parallel to
\vec{p}
in the
(p+q)
rest frame. This statement, however, is incompatible with the
((\vec{c}-\vec{a})\propto\vec{p})
requirement in the third line of (
5.18
).
In all the cases which are compatible with
\{\phantom{M}\}_{D}
we have shown that
\left(\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\right)^{2}-\left(\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\right)^{2}\neq 0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

V_{7}
of (
5.9
) is non-zero when the
C_{3}
constraint of (
302
) is satisfied by an event having one of the following three types:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\text{✓}}&{\text{✗}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\}_{A},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\text{✓}}&{\text{✗}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\}_{B},\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\text{✓}}&{\text{✗}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\circ}&{\text{✓}}&{\circ}\end{array}\right\}_{C}.

Proof.

If we can show the result for
\{\phantom{M}\}_{A}
it will follow for
\{\phantom{M}\}_{B}
and
\{\phantom{M}\}_{C}
by symmetry.
The proof of Lemma
5.17
has already shown that the central column of
\{\phantom{M}\}_{A}
implies that

g^{b}_{p-q}=0

and so Lemma
5.13
tells us that
V_{7}
will be non-zero if we can show that
a^{2}-c^{2}\neq 0
when
\{\phantom{M}\}_{A}
pertains. However this is precisely what the tick in the bottom left corner of
\{\phantom{M}\}_{A}
guarantees.
∎

Lemma 5.20
.

Given the definitions of
V_{6}
and
V_{7}
in (
5.9
) and (
5.9
) respectively, the variables within the set

\displaystyle S_{19}^{(3)}=\{V_{6},V_{7}\}

(347)

assign at least one non-zero parity to every event in
C_{3}
as required.

Proof.

This theorem is just a synthesis of Lemmas
5.19
and

5.18
.
∎

Remark
.

We do not currently use Corollary
5.15
. In principle it would permit
[a,b,c,(p+q)]
to be used in place of the product

\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\cdot F^{abc}_{pq}(3)

in (
5.8
), (
5.9
) and (
5.9
). This substitution would change (and simplify!) the definition of
\mathscr{P}(f)
, which in turn would change (and simplify!) the definitions of
V_{6}
and
V_{7}
. This substitution would not affect the
sufficiency
proofs associated with
V_{6}
and
V_{7}
, however, it complicates the proofs associated with their joint
necessity
and so we therefore avoid making it. It remains possible that the difficulty demonstrating the
irreducibility
of the final set of nineteen variables (when this substitution is made and we have a new
V_{6}
and
V_{7}
) is a sign that this alternative set of 19 variables is not
irreducible
, and that therefore our own
S_{19}
is not a
minimal
set. This line of enquiry should be investigated further.

5.2.5
A set of variables
S_{19}^{(4)}
to cover
C_{4}

Recall that

\displaystyle C_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)=0)\land(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)=0)\right)\land C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)=0)\land(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)=0)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)\neq 0)\lor(\overbrace{{\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\neq 0}}^{\text{FALSE}})

and thus

\displaystyle C_{4}=

(348)

\displaystyle(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}g^{a-b}_{p-q}g^{b-c}_{p-q}g^{c-a}_{p-q}=0)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}

(349)

(350)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(F_{pq}(0)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(2)\neq 0)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{155.24895pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(F_{pq}(0)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(f^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a+b}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta(a-b,p,q)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\

\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
(f^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c+a}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(c-a,p,q)\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{341.5519pt}{1.42262pt}}.

Lemma 5.21
.

C_{4}
of (
348
) is incompatible with
a=b
and is incompatible with
b=c
and is incompatible with
c=a
.

Proof.

Given the
(a,b,c)
-symmetry of
C_{4}
it is sufficient to prove that
C_{4}
is incompatible with
a=b
. Suppose therefore, without loss of generality, that
a=b
. In this case,
\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}=0
and so
\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)=\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=0
.
Independently,
a=b
implies that
\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}=-\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}
. This in turn means that
\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)=\epsilon^{ab}_{pq}+\epsilon^{bc}_{pq}+\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=0-\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}+\epsilon^{ca}_{pq}=0
. These two results (
\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)=0
and
\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)=0
) are in conflict with (
349
) which itself requires that at least one of them be non-zero.
∎

Corollary 5.22
.

We may therefore re-write
C_{4}
in an arguably simpler and more explicit form in which the ‘
a=b
’ options are removed and replaced by explicit (albeit redundant) statements to the contrary:

\displaystyle C_{4}=

(351)

\displaystyle(a\neq b)\land(b\neq c)\land(c\neq a)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}g^{a-b}_{p-q}g^{b-c}_{p-q}g^{c-a}_{p-q}=0)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle\left[(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)\neq 0)\right]\land(\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)=0)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(g^{a-b}_{a+b}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a-b}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a-b}_{p-q}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(g^{b-c}_{b+c}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b-c}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b-c}_{p-q}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(g^{c-a}_{c+a}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c-a}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c-a}_{p-q}\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}

(352)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(F_{pq}(0)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(2)\neq 0)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(F_{pq}(0)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(f^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a+b}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(a-b,p,q)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
(f^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b+c}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(b-c,p,q)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
(f^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c+a}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(c-a,p,q)\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{207.0021pt}{1.42262pt}}.

Remark
.

The present goal is to find a set of parity-odd variables, at least one of which is provably non-zero for any event satisfying
C_{4}
.
One way to do so is to find a set of parity-odd variables which has that same desired property, but on a wider (i.e. less restrictive) class of events. We therefore now define a less restrictive class of events,
C_{4A}
, with a view to finding Parity-odd variables which work for any event satisfying its requirements:

Definition 5.23

(
C_{4}=C_{4A}\land C_{4B}
)
.

Noting that
C_{4}
is a chain of ‘anded’ requirements, we split the constraint into two parts
C_{4A}
and
C_{4B}
such that

C_{4}=C_{4A}\land C_{4B}
:

\displaystyle C_{4A}\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{207.0021pt}{1.42262pt}}\\
(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(g^{a-b}_{a+b}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a-b}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a-b}_{p-q}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(g^{b-c}_{b+c}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b-c}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b-c}_{p-q}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(g^{c-a}_{c+a}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c-a}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c-a}_{p-q}\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(F_{pq}(0)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(2)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{207.0021pt}{1.42262pt}}\end{array}\right]

and

\displaystyle C_{4B}\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}

\displaystyle(a\neq b)\land(b\neq c)\land(c\neq a)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(F^{abc}_{pq}(3)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}g^{a-b}_{p-q}g^{b-c}_{p-q}g^{c-a}_{p-q}=0)\land(\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)=0)

(354)

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}

\displaystyle(F_{pq}(0)\neq 0)\lor(F_{pq}(1)\neq 0)\lor\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(f^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a+b}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(a-b,p,q)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
(f^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b+c}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(b-c,p,q)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{51.7479pt}{0.28453pt}}\\
(f^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c+a}_{p-q}\neq 0)\lor(\Delta_{3}(c-a,p,q)\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{207.0021pt}{1.42262pt}}.

Lemma 5.24
.

If
f(x,y)
is a real or complex valued function which changes sign under exchange of its arguments (i.e.
f(x,y)=-f(y,x)
) then the function

\displaystyle\pi(a,b,c)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}f(a,b)f(b,c)f(c,a)

(355)

is totally antisymmetric in
a
,
b
and
c
. If, furthermore,
f(a,b)\neq 0
,
f(b,c)\neq 0
,
f(c,a)\neq 0
, then
\pi(a,b,c)
is non-zero.

Proof.

Since the function
\pi(a,b,c)
is evidently invariant under cyclic permutations of three labels (
a
,
b
and
c
) it will be totally antisymmetric if it changes sign under the transposition of two of those labels. Without loss of generality, therefore, consider only

\pi(b,a,c)=f(b,a)f(a,c)f(c,b)=-f(a,b)f(c,a)f(b,c)=-\pi(a,b,c)
proving anti-symmetry.
Under the additional conditions stated, the function is evidently non-zero as it is a product of non-zero quantities required. [
NB: Lemma
5.24
is just a special case of Lemma
5.25
. One may therefore prove Lemma
5.24
as a corollary to Lemma
5.25
by simply setting
g(x,y)
equal to
f(x,y)
therein.
]
∎

Lemma 5.25
.

If
f(x,y)
and
g(x,y)
are real or complex valued functions which change sign under exchange of their arguments (i.e.
f(x,y)=-f(y,x)
and
g(x,y)=-g(y,x)
) then the function

\displaystyle\sigma(a,b,c)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}f(a,b)f(b,c)g(c,a)+f(b,c)f(c,a)g(a,b)+f(c,a)f(a,b)g(b,c)

(356)

is totally antisymmetric in
a
,
b
and
c
. If, furthermore,
f(a,b)\neq 0
,
f(b,c)\neq 0
,
f(c,a)=0
and
g(c,a)\neq 0
, then
\sigma(a,b,c)
is non-zero.

Proof.

Since the function
\sigma(a,b,c)
is a sum of terms related by cyclic permutations of three labels (
a
,
b
and
c
) it is totally antisymmetric if it changes sign under the transposition of two of those labels. Without loss of generality, therefore, consider only

\displaystyle\sigma(b,a,c)

\displaystyle=\hphantom{+}f(b,a)f(a,c)g(c,b)+f(a,c)f(c,b)g(b,a)+f(c,b)f(b,a)g(a,c)

\displaystyle=-f(a,b)f(c,a)g(b,c)-f(c,a)f(b,c)g(a,b)-f(b,c)f(a,b)g(c,a)

(antisymmetry:
f,g
)

\displaystyle=-(f(a,b)f(c,a)g(b,c)+f(c,a)f(b,c)g(a,b)+f(b,c)f(a,b)g(c,a))

(factorizing)

\displaystyle=-(f(b,c)f(a,b)g(c,a)+f(c,a)f(b,c)g(a,b)+f(a,b)f(c,a)g(b,c))

(reordering sum)

\displaystyle=-(f(a,b)f(b,c)g(c,a)+f(b,c)f(c,a)g(a,b)+f(c,a)f(a,b)g(b,c))

(commuting products)

\displaystyle=-\sigma(a,b,c)

(357)

proving anti-symmetry.
Under the additional conditions stated,
\sigma(a,b,c)=f(a,b)f(b,c)g(c,a)+0+0=f(a,b)f(b,c)g(c,a)\neq 0
as required.

Remark
.

The proof would fail if
f
and
g
were quaternion-valued functions. This is because quaternions do not always commute, and so the last step in the proof of anti-symmetry would not be valid.

∎

Definition 5.26
.

Define six functions
f_{i}(x)
as follows:

\displaystyle f_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}g^{x}_{x}=\Delta_{2}(x,p+q),

(358)

\displaystyle f_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}g^{x}_{a+b+c}

(359)

\displaystyle f_{3}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}x^{2},

(360)

\displaystyle f_{4}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}g^{x}_{p-q},,

(361)

\displaystyle f_{5}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}\Delta_{2}\left({x},{\Sigma}\right)\equiv\Delta_{2}\left({x},{a+b+c}\right),

(362)

\displaystyle f_{6}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}G\!\left(\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c@{,\ \!}c}{x}&{\Sigma}\\
{p+q}&{\Sigma}\end{array}\!\!\!\!\right)\equiv G\!\left(\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c@{,\ \!}c}{x}&{a+b+c}\\
{p+q}&{a+b+c}\end{array}\!\!\!\!\right)

and let
\delta f_{i}^{xy}\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(y)
denote their differences.

Furthermore, define two complex functions
f_{1\mathbb{C}}(x)
and
f_{2\mathbb{C}}(x)
:

\displaystyle f_{1\mathbb{C}}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}f_{1}(x)+if_{2}(x),

(367)

\displaystyle f_{2\mathbb{C}}(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}f_{3}(x)+if_{4}(x),

(368)

and again let
\delta f_{i\mathbb{C}}(x,y)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}f_{i\mathbb{C}}(x)-f_{i\mathbb{C}}(y)
denote their differences.

Lemma 5.27
.

Using the functions of Definition
5.26
we may write the curly-brace-enclosed constraints of (
5.23
) more symmetrically as either three sets of four real constraints ((
5.27
)) or as three sets of two complex constraints ((
5.27
)):

\displaystyle C_{4A\{\}}

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(g^{a-b}_{a+b}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a-b}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(g^{a-b}_{p-q}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(g^{b-c}_{b+c}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b-c}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(g^{b-c}_{p-q}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(g^{c-a}_{c+a}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c-a}_{a+b+c}\neq 0)\lor(f^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(g^{c-a}_{p-q}\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(\delta f_{1}^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{2}^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{3}^{ab}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{4}^{ab}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(\delta f_{1}^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{2}^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{3}^{bc}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{4}^{bc}\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(\delta f_{1}^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{2}^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{3}^{ca}\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{4}^{ca}\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(\delta f_{1\mathbb{C}}(a,b)\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{2\mathbb{C}}(a,b)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(\delta f_{1\mathbb{C}}(b,c)\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{2\mathbb{C}}(b,c)\neq 0)\\
\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\land\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\rule{0.0pt}{0.56905pt}}\\
(\delta f_{1\mathbb{C}}(c,a)\neq 0)\lor(\delta f_{2\mathbb{C}}(c,a)\neq 0)\end{array}\right\}.

Proof.

The proof of equivalence for column three of (
5.27
) is trivial; the proof for columns two and four follows from the linearity of Gram Determinants; while the proof for column one requires Lemma
B.1
. ∎

Definition 5.28
.

In a similar manner to Definition
5.16
we use shapes like:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\circ&\circ&\text{✗}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\circ&\circ&\circ&\circ\end{array}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-2}\cr\circ&\text{✗}\\
\cline{1-2}\cr\circ&\circ\end{array}\right\}

to represent, respectively, the status of the twelve real constraints in (
5.27
) and the six complex constraints in (
5.27
).

Lemma 5.29
.

Self-consistency dictates that no column of constraints in (
5.28
) can contain one tick and two crosses.

Proof.

Consider, for example, a configuration like:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\circ&\circ&\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\circ&\circ&\text{✗}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\circ&\circ&\text{✗}&\circ\end{array}\right\}.

It asserts that
a^{2}\neq b^{2}
and
b^{2}=c^{2}
and
c^{2}=a^{2}
. This is not self consistent. The same argument works for any column, because in each column every constraint takes the form
\delta f(a,b)\equiv f(a)-f(b)\neq 0
for some
f
.
∎

Corollary 5.30
.

Any event satisfying
C_{4A}
matches at least one of the following ten cases (or row permutations thereof):

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccc}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\end{array}\right\}_{1}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ&\circ\end{array}\right\}_{2}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\end{array}\right\}_{3}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✓}&\circ&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}\end{array}\right\}_{4}\\
\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ&\circ\end{array}\right\}_{5}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ&\circ\\

or equivalently such an event matches at least one of the following three complexly-constrained cases (or row permutations thereof):

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{✓}&\circ\\

Note that case
\{\phantom{i}\}_{10}
, though needed for

, is not needed for

since it is in conflict with the

constraint shown to the left of (

).

Lemma 5.31
.

For any of the ten cases,
\{\phantom{i}\}_{i}
, shown in (
5.30
) (or for any of the three cases,
\{\phantom{i}\}_{i\mathbb{C}}
, shown in (
5.30
)) it is possible to construct a real or complex valued function
f_{i}(a,b,c)
(respectively
f_{i\mathbb{C}}(a,b,c)
) which is totally antisymmetric in
a
,
b
and
c
, and which is never zero when the associated constraint (
\{\phantom{i}\}_{i}
or
\{\phantom{i}\}_{i\mathbb{C}}
) is true.

Proof.

Every case
i
has one of two properties. Either: (i) it has three ticks in a single column - call it column
j
; or (ii) it has two ticks and one cross in one column (call it
j
) and a third tick in a different column (call it
s
) in the same row as the cross in column
j
. Lemmas
5.24
is sufficient to guarantee that for real-constrained cases with ‘property (i)’ then

\displaystyle f_{i}(a,b,c)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}3\delta f_{j}^{ab}\delta f_{j}^{bc}\delta f_{j}^{ca}

(431)

is an antisymmetric function with the desired properties, while Lemma
5.25
is sufficient to guarantee that for real-constrained cases having ‘property (ii)’ that

\displaystyle f_{i}(a,b,c)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}\delta f_{j}^{ab}\delta f_{j}^{bc}\delta f_{s}^{ca}+\delta f_{j}^{ab}\delta f_{s}^{bc}\delta f_{j}^{ca}+\delta f_{s}^{ab}\delta f_{j}^{bc}\delta f_{j}^{ca}

(432)

will work. Similarly, for the complex cases one has correspondingly success with

\displaystyle f_{i\mathbb{C}}(a,b,c)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}3\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{ab}\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{bc}\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{ca}

(433)

and

\displaystyle f_{i\mathbb{C}}(a,b,c)\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{ab}\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{bc}\delta f_{s\mathbb{C}}^{ca}+\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{ab}\delta f_{s\mathbb{C}}^{bc}\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{ca}+\delta f_{s\mathbb{C}}^{ab}\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{bc}\delta f_{j\mathbb{C}}^{ca}.

(434)

∎

Definition 5.32
.

To aid in emphasising commonalities in the above, we can define a universal functional
U^{abc}_{pq}[f(x,y),g_{pq}(x,y)]
, which takes two antisymmetric
31
31

31

By antisymmetric we mean that
f(x,y)=-f(y,x)
and
g_{pq}(x,y)=-g_{pq}(y,x)
.
functions
f(x,y)
and
g_{pq}(x,y)
as inputs, and which returns a totally antisymmetric function of
a
,
b
and
c
. Specifically:

\displaystyle U^{abc}_{pq}[f(x,y),g_{pq}(x,y)]=f(a,b)f(b,c)g_{pq}(c,a)+f(a,b)g_{pq}(b,c)f(c,a)+g(a,b)_{pq}f(b,c)f(c,a).

Note that because
U^{abc}_{pq}
is linear in its second argument,
g_{pq}(x,y)
, it inherits whatever
(pq)
-evenness or
(pq)
-oddness is carried by that second argument. Because we have presented the definition of
U^{abc}_{pq}
using an implicitly
(pq)
-odd function
g_{pq}(x,y)
we have given
U^{abc}_{pq}
a subscript which indicates that inherited symmetry. However
U
could just as easily be used with a
(pq)
-even function
g(x,y)
, in which case it would be denoted
U^{abc}
rather than
U^{abc}_{pq}
.

Definition 5.33
.

\displaystyle{\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u))}

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}{\left(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)\right)^{3}+i{u}\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)},\qquad\text{and}

(435)

\displaystyle{F_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u))}

\displaystyle\underset{\text{def}}{\equiv}{F_{pq}(1)+i{u}F_{pq}(2)}.

(436)

Definition 5.34
.

The following six complex-valued functions:

\displaystyle v[1\mathbb{C}]

\displaystyle={\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u_{2\phantom{1}}))}\cdot{}

\displaystyle U^{abc}_{pq}[\delta f_{1}^{xy}+iu_{1}\delta f_{2}^{xy},\ \ (

\displaystyle\delta f_{1}^{xy}+iu_{1}\delta f_{2}^{xy})\cdot{F_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u_{3\phantom{1}}))}

\displaystyle],

\displaystyle v[2\mathbb{C}]

\displaystyle={\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u_{5\phantom{1}}))}\cdot{}

\displaystyle U^{abc}_{pq}[\delta f_{1}^{xy}+iu_{4}\delta f_{2}^{xy},\ \ (

\displaystyle\delta f_{1}^{xy}+iu_{4}\delta f_{2}^{xy})\cdot F_{pq}(0)

\displaystyle],

\displaystyle v[3\mathbb{C}]

\displaystyle]\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle={\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u_{15}))}\cdot{}

\displaystyle U^{abc}_{pq}[\delta f_{3}^{xy},

\displaystyle\delta f_{3}^{xy}\cdot F_{pq}(0)+iu_{14}\cdot(\delta f_{4}^{xy})_{pq}

\displaystyle]

are defined in terms of fifteen real constants,
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{15}\}
, whose only constraint is that each be non-zero.
In the definition above, the two occurrences of the expression
\delta f_{4}^{xy}
have each been wrapped in subscripted brackets:
\delta f_{4}^{xy}\rightarrow(\delta f_{4}^{xy})_{pq}
. This wrapping is purely to emphasise the
(pq)
-oddness which
\delta f_{4}^{xy}
has and which is not shared by
\delta f_{1}^{xy}
,
\delta f_{2}^{xy}
or
\delta f_{4}^{xy}
; no operation is performed by this bracketing.

Remark

(Purpose of the
u_{i}
)
.

Later on we will be using the twelve real and imaginary parts of
v[1\mathbb{C}]
to
v[6\mathbb{C}]
as real parity-odd event variables which cover
C_{4}
.
The reason for using intermediate complex functions here, even though the ultimate goal is real variables is to permit the use of an efficient ‘trick’ that is exploited in the second bullet point of the proof of Lemma
5.36
. As the remarks at the end of Lemma
B.8
explain in more detail, the ‘magic’ of that trick is that the the algebraic structure of the complex numbers allows a chain of anded pairwise ors to be expressed as no more than two real constraints, and so their use tends to result in fewer real variables at the end. Nonetheless, it is little more than a ‘trick’ and so it should not be a surprise if later a better approach is found that results in a smaller set of event variables. As soon as complex numbers are introduced, the
u_{i}
appear as a ‘gauge freedom’ of sorts – either out of necessity if unit consistency is required – or to permit secondary optimisation. The next remark discusses both of these.

Remark

(Choosing values for
u_{i}
)
.

Where this document requires each
u_{i}
to given a concrete value, we shall set each of them to 1. However, in practical applications it would be sensible to set each
u_{i}
so that it balances the scales and units of the real and imaginary parts of the complex number it is used to build.

For example,
u_{3}
is used to create the complex number

{F_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u_{3}))}={F_{pq}(1)+i{u_{3}}F_{pq}(2)}.

Since
F_{pq}(2)
has the same units as the
cube
of
F_{pq}(1)
, it is likely that setting
u_{3}=1
will lead to
{F_{pq}(1)+i{u_{3}}F_{pq}(2)}
being dominated by either its real or its imaginary part. This is not desirable. Ideally
u_{3}
would be better chosen to be something similar to the ratio of the root mean square values of
F_{pq}(1)
and
F_{pq}(2)
, averaged on the data for which usage is expected. Alternatively the
u_{i}
could simply be treated as hyperparameters for arbitrary optimisation. Although there is nothing wrong with this latter approach, it may still be advisable to initialise any hyperparameter optimisation process with values of the
u_{i}
that set sensible scales (as described above) in order to reduce the chance of that process getting stuck in a local optimum rather than the global optimum.

Yet another approach which can reduce (but not remove) some of the difficulty in finding good values for the
u_{i}
quantities is illustrated by the use of the power three seen in the definition of
{\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u))}
in (
435
). This power at least ensures that
u
is dimensionless, thereby removing a reason for its optimal scale to vary in response to unit-changes in inputs. A similar trick could be applied in (
436
), however inserting a third power there makes it much harder to find the example events needed in Lemma
5.39
when proving that the variables in
S_{19}
are all
necessary
. For that reason we omit an extra power of three in (
436
), however this does not mean that such a definition must be avoided by any end user.

Remark

(Using fewer
u_{i}
)
.

If a user decides to optimise the
u_{i}
for a particular purpose but is concerned about the potential for over training, then it seems likely that the eighteen
u_{i}
values above could perhaps be reduced to four degrees of freedom with little loss of performance by setting the following internal constraints:

\displaystyle u_{1}=u_{4}=u_{6}=u_{9},

(437)

\displaystyle u_{2}=u_{5}=u_{8}=u_{11}=u_{12}=u_{15},

(438)

\displaystyle u_{3}=u_{7}=u_{13},

(439)

\displaystyle u_{10}=u_{14}.

(440)

This reduction may work since each of
u_{1}
,
u_{4}
,
u_{6}
and
u_{9}
exist to scale
\delta f^{xy}_{1}
with respect to
\delta f^{xy}_{2}
and so it seems likely that a common value might work for all of them. Similar arguments may be advanced for the other groups.

Corollary 5.35
.

Each of the six functions in Definition
5.34
is Lorentz-invariant, parity-odd, and invariant with respect to permutations of
(abc)
or
(pq)
.

Proof.

The Lorentz-invariant is trivially inherited from each function’s ingredients. The parity-oddness results from each variable having one and only one parity odd factor — which in all cases has the form
({\left(\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(1)\right)^{3}+i{u}\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(2)})
for some non-zero
u\in\mathbb{R}
.
The invariance with respect to permutations of
(abc)
or
(pq)
may be seen by checking that every product contains both an even number of
(abc)
-odd terms and an even number of
(pq)
-odd terms as indicated by their superscripts or subscripts.
∎

Lemma 5.36
.

With reference to functions written in Definition
5.34
and the brace notation of (
5.30
), the following claims regarding
sufficiency
coverage are made:

•

v[1\mathbb{C}]
and
v[2\mathbb{C}]
collectively cover
\{\phantom{i}\}_{1}
,
\{\phantom{i}\}_{2}
and
\{\phantom{i}\}_{5}
,

•

v[3\mathbb{C}]
and
v[4\mathbb{C}]
collectively cover
\{\phantom{i}\}_{3}
,
\{\phantom{i}\}_{6}
, and
\{\phantom{i}\}_{7}
,

•

v[4\mathbb{C}]
alone covers
\{\phantom{i}\}_{4}
, and

•

v[5\mathbb{C}]
and

v[6\mathbb{C}]

collectively cover
\{\phantom{i}\}_{8}
and
\{\phantom{i}\}_{9}
.

Proof.

The proof appeals to:

•

Lemma
5.31
;

•

the fact that any
n
real or complex numbers are
all
non-zero if and only if their product is non-zero;

•

the fact that
C_{4}
guarantees that
{\mathscr{P}^{abc}_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u))}\neq 0
for any non-zero
u\in\mathbb{R}
;

•

the fact that
C_{4}
guarantees that
\left((F_{pq}(0)\neq 0)\lor({F_{pq}(\mathbb{C}(u))}\neq 0)\right)
for any non-zero
u\in\mathbb{R}
; and

•

the locations within each brace
\{\phantom{i}\}_{i}
from which the ingredients of each function is drawn.

Regarding the last of those bullet points, we present below a visual guide which uses round brackets to show which pairs of real constraints have been combined into a single complex constraint, and which (by their absence) which have not:

•

v[1\mathbb{C}]
and
v[2\mathbb{C}]
both draw from
\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}(\text{✓}&\text{✓})&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr(\text{✓}&\text{✓})&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr(\text{✓}&\text{✓})&\circ&\circ\end{array}\right\}
,

•

v[3\mathbb{C}]
draws from
\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}(\text{✓}&\text{✓})&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr(\text{✓}&\text{✓})&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr(\text{✗}&\text{✗})&\text{✓}&\circ\end{array}\right\}
,

•

v[4\mathbb{C}]
draws from
\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}(\text{✓}&\text{✓})&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr(\text{✓}&\text{✓})&\circ&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr(\text{✗}&\text{✗})&(\text{✓}&\text{✓})\end{array}\right\}
,

•

v[5\mathbb{C}]
draws from
\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\end{array}\right\}
,

•

v[6\mathbb{C}]
draws separately from
\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\end{array}\right\}
and from
\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}&\circ\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✗}&\text{✓}\end{array}\right\}
.

∎

.

If

\displaystyle=\Im[v[1\mathbb{C}]],

(441)

\displaystyle V_{10}

\displaystyle=\Im[v[2\mathbb{C}]],

(442)

\displaystyle V_{13}

then the set

\displaystyle S_{19}^{(4)}=\{V_{8},V_{9},V_{10},V_{11},V_{12},V_{13},V_{14},V_{15},V_{16},V_{17},V_{18},V_{19}\}

(447)

will ascribe at least one non-zero real parity to every

and Lemma

.
∎

5.2.6
The sufficiency and necessity of the variables in
S_{19}

Corollary 5.38

(
Sufficiency
)
.

If the set of parity-odd invariant event variables
S_{19}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle S_{19}

\displaystyle=S_{19}^{(1)}\cup S_{19}^{(2)}\cup S_{19}^{(3)}\cup S_{19}^{(4)}

(448)

in terms of subsets defined in (
291
),
(
292
),
(
347
) and
(
447
), then Lemmas
5.6
,
5.2.3
and
5.20
together with Corollary
5.37
prove that the nineteen variables contained in
S_{19}
will assign at least one non-zero parity to every
chiral

collision event

e\in\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
.

Lemma 5.39

(
Necessity
)
.

Each of the 19 event variables in
S_{19}
can be shown to be
necessary
(at least in the case in which each of the constants
u_{1},\ldots,u_{18}
in
Definition
5.34
are set equal to 1 in whatever unit system is used) by the explicit construction of a
collision event

e\in\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
which results in a non-zero parity for that variable, and a zero-parity for every other variable in
S_{19}
.

Proof.

Below are listed nineteen special
collision events
within
\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
which are named
\text{Event}[1]
to
\text{Event}[19]
. The numbering is such that
\text{Event}[n]
has the property that each of the variables in
\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{19}\}\setminus\{V_{n}\}
evaluates to zero while
V_{n}
alone is non-zero.
\text{Event}[n]
thus serves to demonstrate the necessity of retaining
V_{n}
within
S_{19}
. Note that although the aforementioned properties are independent of the choice of the non-zero real (but otherwise free) constants
u_{1},\ldots,u_{18}
in
Definition
5.34
, some valid values of those constants cause the example events to have complex momenta. Nonetheless, it may be observed that the example events have been chosen such that no event has complex momenta when all those
u_{i}
constants take the value of 1. This is demonstrated (for any events which retain dependence on any
u_{i}
) by showing what form the momenta in those events take for unit
u_{i}
values. It may thus be seen that all example events may, simultaneously, be built of real and timelike momenta in
\mathbb{V}
, as required. Lorentz Vectors in the following list use the display conventions of Appendix
A.2
.

a^{\mu}=\left(\sqrt{2},\left(1,0,0\right)\right),\\
b^{\mu}=\left(\sqrt{2},\left(0,1,0\right)\right),\\
c^{\mu}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{13}}{2},\left(-1,0,0\right)\right)\end{array}\right],

\displaystyle\text{Event}[15]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{l}p^{\mu}=\left(\frac{5}{4},\left(0,0,1\right)\right),\\
q^{\mu}=\left(2,\left(0,0,-1\right)\right),\\
a^{\mu}=\left(\sqrt{3},\left(1,1,0\right)\right),\\
b^{\mu}=\left(\sqrt{2},\left(1,0,0\right)\right),\\
c^{\mu}=\left(\sqrt{11},\left(1,-1,0\right)\right)\end{array}\right],

\displaystyle\text{Event}[16]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{l}p^{\mu}=\left(\frac{3}{2},\left(0,0,1\right)\right),\\

\displaystyle\text{Event}[17]

\displaystyle\text{Event}[18]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{array}[]{l}p^{\mu}=\left(\frac{3}{2},\left(0,0,1\right)\right),\\

∎

Remark
.

Note that since we have shown
necessity
only in the case that the
u_{i}
values are chosen in appropriate ranges (those which keep the event momenta of these particular example events real) then it remains possible that for certain cleverly chosen values of the
u_{i}
some of the variables
V_{k}
might no longer perform tasks not covered by other variables, and so the set may then be
reducible
. Whether this possibility can be used to shrink
S_{19}
to a smaller and more
minimal
set of event variables has not been fully investigated. Nonetheless, preliminary tests suggest that there is nothing to be gained by such a procedure. For example: the only
u_{i}
for which the
u_{i}=1
step of the proof was needed were these:

u_{1}
and
u_{3}

for
V_{8}
and
V_{9}

u_{4}

for
V_{10}

u_{7}
and
u_{8}

for
V_{12}
and
V_{13}

u_{12}
and
u_{13}

for
V_{17}

.

A quick investigation focusing only on the
necessity
of
V_{9}
has shown that for any
(u_{1},u_{3})\neq 0
it is possible to construct an event for which
V_{9}\neq 0
and every other
V_{k}=0
.
32
32

32

No proof of the statement just made is presented in this document. However a demonstration of how one can construct events which make
V_{9}

necessary
for any
(u_{1},u_{3})
may be found in the python reference implementation
reference-implementation.py
supplied with this paper, and a mathematica notebook
parity-variables.nb
which accompanies it provides some background to explain how these reference events were constructed. For more information see Section
5.5
.
The process for constructing such events is tiresome and results in no tangible benefit (beyond confirming that a given
V_{k}
is universally
necessary
) and so the same task has not been re-attempted for the remaining variables
V_{8}
,
V_{10}
.
V_{12}
,
V_{13}
and
V_{17}
. However, the authors do not think it would be impossible to construct such events if the need were strong enough. We therefore make the claim (without proof) that for any
\{u_{k}\}
all the set
S_{19}=\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{19}\}
is
irreducible
on
collision events

e\in\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
.

5.2.7
Simplifications to the set
S_{19}
valid for LHC three photon events

We note, without proof, that of the nineteen variables in
S_{19}
, all but nine of them are non-zero when
m_{p}=m_{q}
(as it is at the LHC) and
m_{a}=m_{b}=m_{c}=0
(as it is if none of the reconstructed particles carry mass information).

An LHC collaboration looking at massless three-jet or three-photon events could therefore use just the nine variables in:

\displaystyle S_{9}=\{V_{1},V_{2},V_{4},V_{5},V_{6},V_{8},V_{9},V_{14},V_{15}\}.

(574)

5.2.8
Discussion concerning the
Minimality
of the set
S_{19}

Remark

(
Minimality
)
.

In Corollary
5.38
and Lemma
5.39

we saw that the variables of
S_{19}
are
necessary
and
sufficient
for the purposes of ascribing parities to
chiral

collision events
in
\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
. But are they
minimal
? Is there a smaller set of variables that can accomplish the same goals?

The simple answer to the above question is that we do not know. However, despite this, there are a number of pieces of circumstantial evidence which suggest that they may not comprise a
minimal
set:

•

In the first remark following the proof of Lemma
5.39
it has already been noted that the possibility has not been excluded that there exist values of
u_{i}
not all equal to 1 (in appropriate units) such that some of the variables in
S_{19}
might be un-
necessary
and the set itself
reducible
.

•

The fact that a
sufficient
set of parity-odd event variables might ever need to contain more than one variable could be said to stem from the fact that any given parity-odd variable can have roots (i.e. can evaluate to zero) on events which are
chiral
as well as
non-chiral
.
33
33

33

Parity-odd variables necessarily evaluate to zero on
non-chiral
events, but this does not preclude them to having zeros on other events.
The greater the order of a polynomial the larger is the number of roots it contains, and correspondingly the greater is the chance that some of these additional unrequested roots will occur on
chiral

events
in
\Omega_{(pq)\rightarrow(abc)X}
rather than in unphysical places. All of the variables contained in
S_{19}
are polynomial functions of momentum components, and many of them have high orders, and so have enlarged chances of having undesirable ‘chiral roots’ – which in turn necessitate the presence of yet more more parity-odd variables to assign parities to those roots. At no point in our argument have we demonstrated that the constructions of our variables represent polynomials with the least possible order – or that they have related beneficial properties. There is therefore no reason to believe that the solution found need be
minimal
.

•

A specific example of the argument just made was already alluded to in the remark following the proof of Lemma
5.20
where it was noted that it had not been possible to demonstrate
irreducibility
for a variant
S_{19}
in which the (lower order) quantity
[a,b,c,(p+q)]
had been used in place of the (much higher order) ingredient
\mathscr{P}_{pq}(3)\cdot F^{abc}_{pq}(3)

in the parity-odd variables covering events in
C_{3}
. This suggests that variables
V_{6}
and
V_{7}
are more complicated than they need to be, or (equivalently) that there is a better partition of
C
into than the
C_{1}\lor C_{2}\lor C_{3}\lor C_{4}
split used here. The reasons that
C_{3}
does not fit well with
[a,b,c,(p+q)]
is that Corollary
5.15
shows only that
C_{3}\implies([a,b,c,(p+q)]\neq 0)
. It does not show that
([a,b,c,(p+q)]\neq 0)\implies C_{3}
. In fact,
[a,b,c,(p+q)]
can even be non-zero on
non-collision
events that are not even in
C
, which is a fact used by all of the variables in (
587
)!

•

The variables in
S_{19}
cover a broader class of events than was originally required, since no use was made of some of the constraints in
C_{4B}
when covering
C_{4}
. It is possible that exploitation of the unused constraints in
C_{4B}
. could allow a smaller set of variables to be created.

•

The complex ‘tricks’ used in Definition
5.34
and discussed in the remarks following it and in Lemma
B.8
, while helpful, seem rather arbitrary. Were there a four-dimensional commutative division algebra (e.g. were the quaternions,
\mathbb{H}
, commutative in multiplication) then we could have used an even more powerful trick – and would have ended up with a smaller set than
S_{19}
. Alas the quaternions are not commutative in multiplication, and we know of no four dimensional alternatives which are.
34
34

34

Frobenius’s Theorem relating to real division algebras presumably prevents any progress in this direction.

Nonetheless, the reduction provided by this ‘trick’ feels wholly accidental and unrelated to the nature of the problem being solved, so it seems unlikely it is the best ‘trick’ that could have been used.

•

Finally (and this is a very subjective point!) the ‘gauge freedom’ presented by the
u_{i}
seems ugly, undesirable and unnecessarily complicated. The
irreducibility
and
sufficiency
properties of the
set
of all variables is unaffected by the
u_{i}
values, and yet the individual parities are strongly influenced by the
u_{i}
. This does not seem to be a welcome state of affairs.

In summary: it would not be surprising to discover that a set of event variables could be constructed which was smaller than
S_{19}
and yet shared all its other desired properties.

.

The nine variables in the set

of (

)
assign at least one non-zero real parity to every

to be considered.

5.4
Events

Sufficiency of the variables in

.

If the set of parity-odd invariant event variables
S_{28}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle S_{28}

\displaystyle=S_{19}^{(1)}\cup S_{19}^{(2)}\cup S_{19}^{(3)}\cup S_{19}^{(4)}\cup S^{(\text{non-coll})}

(588)

in terms of subsets defined in (

),
(

),

),
(

) and

), then Lemmas

,

and

together with Corollaries

and

prove that the twenty-eight variables in
